````text
Step 1: Calculate Basic Financial Metrics
Sales Revenue = 900 units × ₹30 = ₹27,000
Variable Costs = 900 units × ₹15 = ₹13,500
Contribution = Sales - Variable Costs = ₹27,000 - ₹13,500 = ₹13,500
Situation X with Financial Plan C
Operating Leverage Calculation:
Fixed Costs (Situation X): ₹6,000
EBIT = Contribution - Fixed Costs = ₹13,500 - ₹6,000 = ₹7,500
Operating Leverage (OL) = Contribution / EBIT = ₹13,500 / ₹7,500 = 1.80
Financial Leverage Calculation:
Interest on 12% Debentures = 12% × ₹20,000 = ₹2,400
EBT = EBIT - Interest = ₹7,500 - ₹2,400 = ₹5,100
Financial Leverage (FL) = EBIT / EBT = ₹7,500 / ₹5,100 = 1.47
Combined Leverage = OL × FL = 1.80 × 1.47 = 2.65
Situation Y with Financial Plan D
Operating Leverage Calculation:
Fixed Costs (Situation Y): ₹8,000

1 b.
Capital Budgeting is the process of planning, evaluating, and selecting long-term investment projects
that involve significant capital expenditure. It involves deciding which fixed assets or projects a
company should invest in, such as purchasing new machinery, expanding facilities, launching new
products, or acquiring another business.
The term "capital" refers to the long-term funds invested, while "budgeting" refers to the planning and
allocation of these funds to maximise shareholder wealth.
Importance of Capital Budgeting in Financial Management
1. Long-Term Implications
Capital investment decisions commit substantial funds for extended periods, directly affecting the
company's future profitability and growth trajectory.
2. Large Financial Commitment
These decisions involve significant amounts of money that, once committed, cannot be easily reversed
without substantial loss.
3. Risk Management
Capital budgeting helps assess and manage the risks associated with long-term investments, protecting
the company from poor investment choices.
4. Strategic Alignment
It ensures that investment decisions align with the company's overall strategic goals and competitive
positioning.
5. Resource Optimization
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
With limited capital resources, proper evaluation ensures funds are allocated to the most profitable and
viable projects.
6. Shareholder Wealth Maximization
Sound capital budgeting decisions enhance the company's value and generate returns that exceed the
cost of capital.
7. Irreversibility
Many capital investments are irreversible or can only be reversed at significant cost, making the initial
decision critical.
8. Competitive Advantage
Investing in the right projects at the right time can provide a sustainable competitive advantage in the
market.
1 c. Real Assets
Real Assets are tangible or physical assets that have intrinsic value due to their substance and
properties. They represent productive capacity and are used to produce goods and services.
Examples: Land, buildings, machinery, equipment, inventory, commodities (gold, oil), infrastructure,
patents, trademarks, and intellectual property.
Financial Assets
Financial Assets are intangible assets that derive their value from a contractual claim. They represent
ownership or a creditor relationship and do not have physical substance.
Examples: Stocks, bonds, mutual funds, bank deposits, debentures, treasury bills, derivatives, and
insurance policies.

i. Cost of Redeemable Preference Share Capital (Kp)
The formula for the Cost of Redeemable Preference Share Capital is:
Kp=Dividend + (Redemption value – Sales Proceeds)/n/(Redemption value – Sales Proceeds)/2
1. Calculate Components:
• Face Value (FV): ₹100
Annual Preference Dividend (PD): 10% of FV = 0.10×₹100=₹10
Net Issue Price (P0): ₹96
Redemption Value (RV): Face Value + Premium =₹100+(5% of ₹100)=₹100+₹5=₹105
Number of Years (n): 7 years
Substitute Values into the Formula:
Kp=₹10+(₹105−₹96)/7/(₹105+₹96)/2×100
Kp = 11.23%
ii. Cost of Irredeemable Debentures (Kd)
Kd=Annual Interest/Net Issue Proceeds ×100
=Kd=₹120/₹980×100
Kd = 12.24%
2 a. Working Capital is mathematically defined as the difference between a company's current assets
and its current liabilities.
Interpreting the Result
1. Positive Working Capital (Current Assets is greater than Current Liabilities): This indicates that
a company has sufficient liquid assets to cover its short-term debts. It is generally a sign of good
liquidity and financial stability.
2. Negative Working Capital (Current Assets is lesser than Current Liabilities): This means the
company's short-term debts are greater than its short-term assets. This is often a sign of poor
liquidity and potential risk of not being able to meet its immediate obligations, potentially
leading to a financial crisis.
Factors Affecting Working Capital Requirements
The amount of working capital a business requires varies significantly based on both internal and
external factors
1. Nature of the business
2. Scale of operations
3. Operating cycle
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
4. Credit Policy
5. Inventory Management
6. Growth and Expansion
7. Seasonal factors
8. Business Cycle
9. Terms of Credit from Suppliers
10. Price Level Changes (Inflation)
2 b. The optimal dividend payout ratio under Walter's Model is determined by comparing the firm's
internal rate of return (r) with its cost of equity (k).
Comparison: Since r=15% and k=10%, we have r is greater than k
Optimal Payout Ratio: The optimal dividend payout ratio is 0% (zero).
Explanation: When the firm's internal rate of return (r) is greater than the cost of equity (k), it means the
company can earn a higher return on its retained earnings (15%) than the shareholders can earn by
investing the dividends elsewhere (10%). Such a company is considered a Growth Firm.
To maximise the shareholders' wealth (i.e., the market price per share, P), the company should retain all
its earnings for reinvestment and pay out a 0% dividend. The shareholders are better off with the
company retaining the earnings to achieve a higher rate of return, which increases the overall value of
the firm and, consequently, the market price of the share.
(ii) Calculation of Theoretical Market Price per Share
Walter's Model formula for the market price per share (P) is:
P=[D+r/k(E−D)]/k
i. 50% Payout Ratio
1. Dividend per Share (D):
D=E×Payout Ratio=₹10×50%=₹5.00
P=5+0.15/0.10 (10-5)/0.10
P=12.50/0.10
P=₹125.00
ii. 100% Payout Ratio
Dividend per Share (D):
D=E×Payout Ratio=₹10×100%=₹10.00
P=10+0.15/0.10 (10-10)/0.10
P = ₹100.00
iii. 0% Payout Ratio
Dividend per Share (D):
D=E×Payout Ratio=₹10×0%=₹0.00
P=0+0.15/0.10 (10-0)/0.10
P = ₹150.00

Discounted Payback Period (DPP)
The Discounted Payback Period is the time taken for the cumulative discounted cash inflows to equal
the initial cash outlay (ICOL of ₹ 9,50,000).
From the cumulative column in the table above:
• The cumulative inflow at the end of Year 3 is ₹ 8,37,158.
• The initial outlay remaining to be recovered is:
Balance ICOL=₹9,50,000−₹8,37,158=₹1,12,842
• This balance is recovered during Year 4 by the discounted cash flow of ₹ 2,21,086
Fraction of Year 4=Balance ICOL/Discounted CFAT of Year 4 =₹2,21,086₹1,12,842=0.5104 years
DPP=3 years+0.5104 years =3.51 years

6 a. 1. Calculation of Specific Costs of Capital
A. Cost of Equity (Ke)
We'll use the Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM) formula:
Ke=Rf+β(Rm−Rf)
Where:
• Rf (Risk-free rate) = 6%
• β (Beta) = 1.25
• (Rm−Rf) (Market risk premium) = 7%
Ke=6%+1.25(7%)
Ke=6%+8.75%=14.75%
B. Cost of Preference Share Capital (Kp)
This is the cost of redeemable preference shares, calculated using the formula:
Kp=PD+(RV−NP)/n/(RV+NP)/2
Where:
• PD (Annual Preference Dividend) =₹100×11%=₹11
• RV (Redemption Value, assumed par) =₹100
• NP (Net Proceeds/Current Market Price) =₹102
• n (Redemption Period) =6 years
Kp=11+(100−102)/6/(100+102)/2
Kp≈10.56%
C. Cost of Debentures (Kd)
This is the cost of redeemable debentures, calculated using the formula:
Kd=I(1−t)+(RV−NP)/n/(RV+NP)/2
Where:
•   I (Annual Interest) =₹1,000×10%=₹100
•   t (Tax Rate) =30% or 0.30
• RV (Redemption Value, assumed par) =₹1,000
• NP (Net Proceeds/Current Market Price) =₹965
• n (Redemption Period) =4 years
Kd=(100(1−0.30)+(1,000−965)/4/1,000+965)/2
Kd=8.02%
D. Cost of Term Loans (Kt)
The term loans are 9% and are generally assumed to be perpetual with no transaction costs, so the cost
is:
Kt=Interest Rate(1−t)=9%(1−0.30)=9%×0.70=6.30%
Reserves and Surplus are treated as retained earnings, and their cost is Kr=Ke=14.75%. Equity Share
Capital, Reserves, and Surplus are combined to calculate the weight of equity.

6b.
Financial markets facilitate the efficient allocation of funds by connecting savers (surplus units) with
borrowers (deficit units), ensuring capital flows to its most productive uses. They achieve this primarily
through three critical functions: information dissemination (price discovery), liquidity provision, and
reduced transaction costs.
1. Price Discovery and Information Dissemination
Financial markets act as a central hub where all available information about companies, industries,
and the economy is aggregated.
•   Price Signal: The market price of a financial asset (like a stock or bond) reflects the collective
judgment of all participants about the asset's expected future cash flows and associated risk. This
process is called price discovery.
•   Efficient Allocation: This price acts as a signal to the entire economy. Firms with strong growth
prospects and high expected returns will have higher stock or bond prices, signalling to investors
that they are the most productive place for capital. Conversely, poorly managed or low-growth firms
will see lower valuations, discouraging further investment. This ensures capital is allocated to its
highest-value use.
2. Liquidity Provision
Liquidity is the ease with which an asset can be converted into cash without a significant loss in
value. Financial markets are essential providers of this feature.
• Encouraging Investment: Investors are more willing to commit their savings to long-term projects
(like corporate expansion or infrastructure) if they know they can easily sell their financial
instruments (stocks or bonds) at a fair price whenever they need cash.
• Matching Maturities: By ensuring a secondary market for trading, financial markets allow firms to
raise long-term capital while letting investors maintain the flexibility of short-term access to their
funds. This function is vital for financing long-gestation economic projects.
3. Risk Sharing and Transaction Cost Reduction
Financial markets employ various mechanisms to reduce the barriers to capital transfer.
• Risk Transformation: Markets allow risk to be shared and redistributed among participants. Different
financial instruments (e.g., high-risk equities vs. low-risk government bonds) cater to various risk
tolerances. Furthermore, derivatives markets enable firms to hedge specific risks (like currency or
commodity price volatility), making their core business cash flows more predictable and attractive to
investors.
********** 11 **********
--- page 12 ---
• Lowering Costs: By providing standardised financial contracts and centralised trading platforms (like
exchanges), financial markets dramatically reduce the search and information costs and the
transaction costs associated with transferring funds. This efficiency makes it cheaper for businesses
to raise capital and cheaper for investors to allocate it.
````

````text
BL-   generate an additional net cash flow of Rs. 300 per unit sold. Assuming that the same
Apply   number of units will be sold each year over the five-year life of the project (assume
end-of-year sales), determine how many units must be sold each year to enable the
company to pay the equated annual installment of the loan over the five-year period.
Solution:
Total Investment in new technology = 2000000
Life of the technology/investment = 5 years
Interest rate on loan/discount rate = 12%
Cash flow generated per unit = 300
Equated annual installment = 2000000 / PVIFA (12%, 5 years)
PVA(12%, 5 years) = A {[(1+r) ^n - 1]/ (r * (1+r) ^n)}
Hence, 2000000 = A * 3.6047
Equated Annual Installment = 2000000/3.6047 = 554819.46   (2 Marks)
Number of Units to be sold each year = Equated Annual Installment / Cash Flow per
unit
= 554819.46/300 = 1849.40   or 1850 units   (1 Marks)

Operating Risk (DOL) : Novex Ltd.: 2.00 > Zentix Ltd.: 1.67
Novex Ltd. has higher operating risk due to higher fixed cost proportion. Its EBIT is
more sensitive to changes in sales.
Financial Risk (DFL) : Zentix Ltd.: 1.38 > Novex Ltd.: 1.29
Zentix Ltd. has higher financial risk because of higher interest burden.
Overall / Combined Risk (DCL) : Novex Ltd.: 2.57 > Zentix Ltd.: 2.31.
Novex Ltd. has higher total (combined) risk, meaning EPS is more sensitive to
changes in sales.

a How do financial markets contribute to the growth of an economy?
Solution:
•   Financial markets facilitate capital formation by enabling companies and
Q7   governments to raise funds through shares and bonds for investment and
expansion.
CO-1;   • They help in facilitating investment by providing a platform where
SO-;   individuals and institutions can invest their savings in instruments like   [05
BL-   stocks, bonds, and mutual funds.
]
•   Financial markets ensure price determination, where the prices of financial
Remembe
assets are decided based on demand and supply, reflecting market
r   expectations.
• They provide liquidity by allowing investors to easily buy and sell securities
without major price fluctuations.
•   Financial markets support risk management through instruments like
derivatives, which help in hedging against risks such as price changes,
interest rate fluctuations, and currency movements.
b   "Preference shares combine certain features of both equity and debt, providing
investors with preferential rights while allowing companies flexibility in financing."
With reference to the above statement, explain the key features of preference share
financing
Solution: (Any five points may be considered of 1 mark each)
Claims   on   Income   and   Assets
Preference shareholders have a prior claim over equity shareholders on dividends and
on the company’s assets at the time of liquidation.
2.   Fixed   Dividend
Preference shares carry a fixed rate of dividend which is paid before any dividend is
Q7
distributed to equity shareholders.
CO-1;
SO-;   3.   Cumulative   Dividend [05
BL-   If the company is unable to pay dividends in a particular year, the unpaid dividends
accumulate and must be paid in future years before equity dividends are paid.   ]
Remembe
4.   Redemption
r   Preference shares are usually redeemable, meaning the company repays the capital to
shareholders after a specified period.
5.   Sinking   Fund
A sinking fund is a reserve created by the company to accumulate funds over time for
redeeming preference shares at maturity.
6.   Call   Feature
A call feature gives the company the right to redeem the preference shares before the
scheduled redemption date.
7.   Participation   Feature
Participating preference shareholders have the right to receive additional dividends
along with equity shareholders after the fixed dividend is paid.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
8.   Voting   Rights
Preference shareholders generally do not have voting rights, except in certain
situations such as when dividends remain unpaid for a specified period.
9.   Convertibility
Convertible preference shares can be converted into equity shares after a specified
time or under predetermined conditions.
********** 9 **********
````

````text
a How do financial markets contribute to the growth of an economy?
Solution:
•   Financial markets facilitate capital formation by enabling companies and
Q7   governments to raise funds through shares and bonds for investment and
expansion.
CO-1;   • They help in facilitating investment by providing a platform where
SO-;   individuals and institutions can invest their savings in instruments like   [05
BL-   stocks, bonds, and mutual funds.
]
•   Financial markets ensure price determination, where the prices of financial
Remembe
assets are decided based on demand and supply, reflecting market
r   expectations.
• They provide liquidity by allowing investors to easily buy and sell securities
without major price fluctuations.
•   Financial markets support risk management through instruments like
derivatives, which help in hedging against risks such as price changes,
interest rate fluctuations, and currency movements.
b   "Preference shares combine certain features of both equity and debt, providing
investors with preferential rights while allowing companies flexibility in financing."
With reference to the above statement, explain the key features of preference share
financing
Solution: (Any five points may be considered of 1 mark each)
Claims   on   Income   and   Assets
Preference shareholders have a prior claim over equity shareholders on dividends and
on the company’s assets at the time of liquidation.
2.   Fixed   Dividend
Preference shares carry a fixed rate of dividend which is paid before any dividend is
Q7
distributed to equity shareholders.
CO-1;
SO-;   3.   Cumulative   Dividend [05
BL-   If the company is unable to pay dividends in a particular year, the unpaid dividends
accumulate and must be paid in future years before equity dividends are paid.   ]
Remembe
4.   Redemption
r   Preference shares are usually redeemable, meaning the company repays the capital to
shareholders after a specified period.
5.   Sinking   Fund
A sinking fund is a reserve created by the company to accumulate funds over time for
redeeming preference shares at maturity.
6.   Call   Feature
A call feature gives the company the right to redeem the preference shares before the
scheduled redemption date.
7.   Participation   Feature
Participating preference shareholders have the right to receive additional dividends
along with equity shareholders after the fixed dividend is paid.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
8.   Voting   Rights
Preference shareholders generally do not have voting rights, except in certain
situations such as when dividends remain unpaid for a specified period.
9.   Convertibility
Convertible preference shares can be converted into equity shares after a specified
time or under predetermined conditions.
********** 9 **********
````

````text
b   Phoenix Ltd. has invested Rs. 20,00,000 in a new automated production system. The
CO- 2;   03
entire investment was financed through a loan carrying an interest rate of 12%
SO- ;   .

********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
BL-   generate an additional net cash flow of Rs. 300 per unit sold. Assuming that the same
Apply   number of units will be sold each year over the five-year life of the project (assume
end-of-year sales), determine how many units must be sold each year to enable the
company to pay the equated annual installment of the loan over the five-year period.
Solution:
Total Investment in new technology = 2000000
Life of the technology/investment = 5 years
Interest rate on loan/discount rate = 12%
Cash flow generated per unit = 300
Equated annual installment = 2000000 / PVIFA (12%, 5 years)
PVA(12%, 5 years) = A {[(1+r) ^n - 1]/ (r * (1+r) ^n)}
Hence, 2000000 = A * 3.6047
Equated Annual Installment = 2000000/3.6047 = 554819.46   (2 Marks)
Number of Units to be sold each year = Equated Annual Installment / Cash Flow per
unit
= 554819.46/300 = 1849.40   or 1850 units   (1 Marks)

Operating Risk (DOL) : Novex Ltd.: 2.00 > Zentix Ltd.: 1.67
Novex Ltd. has higher operating risk due to higher fixed cost proportion. Its EBIT is
more sensitive to changes in sales.
Financial Risk (DFL) : Zentix Ltd.: 1.38 > Novex Ltd.: 1.29
Zentix Ltd. has higher financial risk because of higher interest burden.
Overall / Combined Risk (DCL) : Novex Ltd.: 2.57 > Zentix Ltd.: 2.31.
Novex Ltd. has higher total (combined) risk, meaning EPS is more sensitive to
changes in sales.
````

````text
changes in sales.

a How do financial markets contribute to the growth of an economy?
Solution:
•   Financial markets facilitate capital formation by enabling companies and
Q7   governments to raise funds through shares and bonds for investment and
expansion.
CO-1;   • They help in facilitating investment by providing a platform where
SO-;   individuals and institutions can invest their savings in instruments like   [05
BL-   stocks, bonds, and mutual funds.
]
•   Financial markets ensure price determination, where the prices of financial
Remembe
assets are decided based on demand and supply, reflecting market
r   expectations.
• They provide liquidity by allowing investors to easily buy and sell securities
without major price fluctuations.
•   Financial markets support risk management through instruments like
derivatives, which help in hedging against risks such as price changes,
interest rate fluctuations, and currency movements.
b   "Preference shares combine certain features of both equity and debt, providing
investors with preferential rights while allowing companies flexibility in financing."
With reference to the above statement, explain the key features of preference share
financing
Solution: (Any five points may be considered of 1 mark each)
Claims   on   Income   and   Assets
Preference shareholders have a prior claim over equity shareholders on dividends and
on the company’s assets at the time of liquidation.
2.   Fixed   Dividend
Preference shares carry a fixed rate of dividend which is paid before any dividend is
Q7
distributed to equity shareholders.
CO-1;
SO-;   3.   Cumulative   Dividend [05
BL-   If the company is unable to pay dividends in a particular year, the unpaid dividends
accumulate and must be paid in future years before equity dividends are paid.   ]
Remembe
4.   Redemption
r   Preference shares are usually redeemable, meaning the company repays the capital to
shareholders after a specified period.
5.   Sinking   Fund
A sinking fund is a reserve created by the company to accumulate funds over time for
redeeming preference shares at maturity.
6.   Call   Feature
A call feature gives the company the right to redeem the preference shares before the
scheduled redemption date.
7.   Participation   Feature
Participating preference shareholders have the right to receive additional dividends
along with equity shareholders after the fixed dividend is paid.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
8.   Voting   Rights
Preference shareholders generally do not have voting rights, except in certain
situations such as when dividends remain unpaid for a specified period.
9.   Convertibility
Convertible preference shares can be converted into equity shares after a specified
time or under predetermined conditions.
********** 9 **********
````

````text
b   Phoenix Ltd. has invested Rs. 20,00,000 in a new automated production system. The
CO- 2;   03
entire investment was financed through a loan carrying an interest rate of 12%
SO- ;   .
compounded annually. With the introduction of this system, the company expects to
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
BL-   generate an additional net cash flow of Rs. 300 per unit sold. Assuming that the same
Apply   number of units will be sold each year over the five-year life of the project (assume
end-of-year sales), determine how many units must be sold each year to enable the
company to pay the equated annual installment of the loan over the five-year period.
Solution:
Total Investment in new technology = 2000000
Life of the technology/investment = 5 years
Interest rate on loan/discount rate = 12%
Cash flow generated per unit = 300
Equated annual installment = 2000000 / PVIFA (12%, 5 years)
PVA(12%, 5 years) = A {[(1+r) ^n - 1]/ (r * (1+r) ^n)}
Hence, 2000000 = A * 3.6047
Equated Annual Installment = 2000000/3.6047 = 554819.46   (2 Marks)
Number of Units to be sold each year = Equated Annual Installment / Cash Flow per
unit
= 554819.46/300 = 1849.40   or 1850 units   (1 Marks)

a How do financial markets contribute to the growth of an economy?
Solution:
•   Financial markets facilitate capital formation by enabling companies and
Q7   governments to raise funds through shares and bonds for investment and
expansion.
CO-1;   • They help in facilitating investment by providing a platform where
SO-;   individuals and institutions can invest their savings in instruments like   [05
BL-   stocks, bonds, and mutual funds.
]
•   Financial markets ensure price determination, where the prices of financial
Remembe
assets are decided based on demand and supply, reflecting market
r   expectations.
• They provide liquidity by allowing investors to easily buy and sell securities
without major price fluctuations.
•   Financial markets support risk management through instruments like
derivatives, which help in hedging against risks such as price changes,
interest rate fluctuations, and currency movements.
b   "Preference shares combine certain features of both equity and debt, providing
investors with preferential rights while allowing companies flexibility in financing."
With reference to the above statement, explain the key features of preference share
financing
Solution: (Any five points may be considered of 1 mark each)
Claims   on   Income   and   Assets
Preference shareholders have a prior claim over equity shareholders on dividends and
on the company’s assets at the time of liquidation.
2.   Fixed   Dividend
Preference shares carry a fixed rate of dividend which is paid before any dividend is
Q7
distributed to equity shareholders.
CO-1;
SO-;   3.   Cumulative   Dividend [05
BL-   If the company is unable to pay dividends in a particular year, the unpaid dividends
accumulate and must be paid in future years before equity dividends are paid.   ]
Remembe
4.   Redemption
r   Preference shares are usually redeemable, meaning the company repays the capital to
shareholders after a specified period.
5.   Sinking   Fund
A sinking fund is a reserve created by the company to accumulate funds over time for
redeeming preference shares at maturity.
6.   Call   Feature
A call feature gives the company the right to redeem the preference shares before the
scheduled redemption date.
7.   Participation   Feature
Participating preference shareholders have the right to receive additional dividends
along with equity shareholders after the fixed dividend is paid.
********** 8 **********
--- page 9 ---
8.   Voting   Rights
Preference shareholders generally do not have voting rights, except in certain
situations such as when dividends remain unpaid for a specified period.
9.   Convertibility
Convertible preference shares can be converted into equity shares after a specified
time or under predetermined conditions.
********** 9 **********
````

````text
Optimum Dividend Policy using Walter’s Model
(5 Marks)
Given:
E = ₹40 crore / 40 lakh shares = ₹100 per share
k = 13%
r = 22%
Since r > k (22% > 13%), as per Walters’s Model:
The firm should retain earnings
Dividend payout ratio should be 0%
Optimum Policy = 0% dividend payout (retain all earnings)
Q7   Note on Walters’s Model
CO-2; SO-;   (5 Marks)   [10]
BL- Apply   Walter’s Model states that dividend policy is relevant and
affects the value of the firm. It emphasizes the relationship
between the firm’s internal rate of return (r) and cost of equity
(k).
The model suggests:
If r > k → Retain earnings (low/zero dividend)
If r < k → Pay higher dividends
If r = k → Dividend policy is irrelevant
It assumes no external financing, constant r and k, and that all
earnings are either retained or distributed.
Thus, Walter’s Model helps in determining the optimal
dividend payout ratio to maximize shareholders’ wealth.
********** 5 **********
````

````text
1 b. Objectives of Cash Management in a Business Organisation
1. Ensuring Sufficient Liquidity:
Maintain adequate cash or easily convertible assets to meet day-to-day operational expenses and
********** 1 **********
--- page 2 ---
liabilities on time without disruption. This prevents payment delays to suppliers, employees, and
creditors.
2. Minimising Borrowing Costs:
Avoid unnecessary short-term borrowings by maintaining optimum cash balances that meet
current requirements and reduce interest expenses on emergency loans.
3. Optimising Returns on Idle Cash:
Invest surplus cash in short-term, low-risk instruments to earn returns on otherwise idle funds,
enhancing profitability.
4. Planning for Expected and Unexpected Cash Needs:
Forecast cash flows accurately to prepare for regular business cycles and cushion against
unforeseen expenses or emergencies.
5. Maintaining Financial Control:
Regular monitoring and control of cash inflows and outflows help prevent fraud, misuse, or
errors, and aid in better decision-making.
6. Avoiding Insolvency and Maintaining Goodwill:
Efficient cash management prevents cash shortages that might lead to payment defaults,
protecting the company's creditworthiness and reputation.

1 d.
(i) Degree of Operating Leverage (DOL)
Given:
•   Sales Revenue = ₹12,00,000
• Variable Costs = ₹7,20,000
• Fixed Costs = ₹2,40,000
• EBIT = ₹2,40,000
First, Contribution Margin:
********** 2 **********
--- page 3 ---
Contribution Margin=12,00,000−7,20,000=₹4,80,000
Now, DOL:
DOL=4,80,000/2,40,000=2.0
Interpretation: A 1% change in sales will result in a 2% change in EBIT.
(ii) Degree of Financial Leverage (DFL)
Formula:
DFL=EBIT/EBIT−Interest
Given:
• EBIT = ₹2,40,000
•   Interest = ₹70,000
DFL=2,40,000/2,40,000−70,000=2,40,000/1,70,000 = 1.41
Interpretation: A 1% change in EBIT will result in approximately a 1.41% change in net earnings before
tax.
(iii) Degree of Combined Leverage (DCL)
Formula:
DCL=DOL×DFL
Or
DCL=Contribution Margin/EBIT−Interest
Let's use the full formula:
DCL=4,80,000/2,40,000−70,000=4,80,000/1,70,000 = 2.82
Interpretation: A 1% change in sales will result in approximately a 2.82% change in net earnings before
tax.

2 b. Financial institutions are essential pillars of a modern economy, facilitating savings, investment,
credit, and risk management—all of which contribute significantly to national economic development.
Role and Importance of Financial Institutions in Economic Development
• Mobilization and Allocation of Savings: Financial institutions channel household and corporate
savings into investments for industry and infrastructure, promoting capital formation and
productive growth.
********** 3 **********
--- page 4 ---
•   Facilitating Investment and Entrepreneurship: By providing credit, venture capital, and financial
services to businesses—including small and medium enterprises—these institutions fuel
entrepreneurial activity and technological progress.
• Risk Management: Banks and insurers help businesses and individuals manage financial risks
through diversified portfolios, insurance products, and prudent lending practices, supporting
economic resiliency.
•   Efficient Payment and Settlement Systems: They establish and operate the financial
infrastructure necessary for smooth, secure, and efficient payments and transfers throughout the
economy, thereby supporting business efficiency and trade.
• Implementation of Monetary and Fiscal Policy: Central and development banks serve as
instruments for government monetary policy, regulating money supply and aiding economic
stability and policy objectives.
• Promotion of Financial Inclusion: By extending financial products (like microfinance) to
underserved populations, financial institutions advance inclusive growth—reducing poverty and
enabling broader economic participation.
•   Fostering Economic Stability: By following regulatory standards and prudent risk management,
financial institutions help maintain economic stability and public confidence during financial
crises.
3 a.
(i) Value of the Firm and Cost of Equity
Given:
• Net Operating Income (NOI) / EBIT: ₹1,20,000
• Outstanding Debentures: ₹6,00,000 @ 7%
•   Overall Capitalisation Rate (Ko): 12%
1. Value of the Firm
The NOI approach states:
Value of Firm (V)=NOI/Ko
=1,20,000/0.12=₹10,00,000
2. Value of Debt (D)
D=₹6,00,000
3. Value of Equity (S)
S=V−D=10,00,000−6,00,000=₹4,00,000
4. Earnings for Equity (Net Income)
Interest on Debentures: 6,00,000×7%=₹42,000
Net Income to Equity: 1,20,000−42,000=₹78,000
5. Cost of Equity (Ke)
Ke=Net Income to Equity/S=78,000/4,00,000=0.195=19.5%
(b) Additional Debentures Issued (Debt = ₹9,00,000)
Value of Equity (S)
S=V−D=10,00,000−9,00,000=₹1,00,000
Interest on Debentures:9,00,000×7%=₹63,000
Net Income to Equity:1,20,000−63,000=₹57,000
Cost of Equity: Ke=57,000/1,00,000=0.57=57%
Effect
• Firm value remains constant at ₹10,00,000.
• Cost of equity rises sharply to 57% due to higher risk for equity holders as debt portion
increases.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
(c) Debt Reduced to ₹4,00,000
Value of Equity (S)
S=10,00,000−4,00,000=₹6,00,000S=10,00,000−4,00,000=₹6,00,000
Interest:4,00,000×7%=₹28,000
Net Income to Equity:1,20,000−28,000=₹92,000
Cost of Equity: Ke=92,000/6,00,000 =0.1533=15.33%
Effect
• Firm value remains constant at ₹10,00,000.
• Cost of equity falls as debt declines and the equity base becomes safer.

4 b.
Earnings available to equity = Net Profit – Preference Dividend
Preference Dividend = ₹80 lakhs × 10% = ₹8 lakhs
Earnings to Equity = ₹40 lakhs – ₹8 lakhs = ₹32 lakhs
Earnings and Dividend per Share
Number of equity shares = 4 lakhs
Earnings Per Share (EPS) = ₹32 lakhs / 4 lakhs = ₹8
Dividend per share (D) for each payout ratio (D = DPS):
• Case (i): 30% payout, so D = 0.30 × ₹8 = ₹2.40
• Case (ii): 60% payout, so D = 0.60 × ₹8 = ₹4.80
• Case (iii): 100% payout, so D = ₹8
Retention Ratio (b) and Growth (g)
Retention Ratio (b) = 1 – Dividend payout ratio
Growth rate (g) = b × r = Retention Ratio × Return on Investment
• Case (i): b = 70%, so g = 0.70 × 0.18 = 0.126 = 12.6%
• Case (ii): b = 40% , so g = 0.40 × 0.18 = 0.072 = 7.2%
• Case (iii): b = 0%, so g = 0 × 0.18 = 0%
Gordon’s Model:
P=D/Ke−g
(i) Dividend payout = 30%
• D = ₹2.40, Ke = 15%, g = 12.6%
P=2.40/0.15−0.126 =2.40/0.024=₹100
(ii) Dividend payout = 60%
• D = ₹4.80, Ke = 15%, g = 7.2%
P=4.80/0.15−0.072=4.80/0.078 = ₹61.54
(iii) Dividend payout = 100%
• D = ₹8, Ke = 15%, g = 0
P=8/0.15−0=8/0.15 = ₹53.33

********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
5 a.
Let's analyse the two investment projects, A and B, by calculating the Accounting Rate of Return (ARR)
and the Payback Period for each.
Particulars   Project   Project B ₹
A ₹
Initial Investment   2,50,000   3,00,000
Year 1 Net Cash   60,000   80,000
Inflow
Year 2 Net Cash   70,000   90,000
Inflow
Year 3 Net Cash   80,000   1,00,000
Inflow
Year 4 Net Cash   90,000   1,20,000
Inflow
Average Annual Profit = (Total Net Cash Inflows - Total Depreciation) / Project Life
•   Total Net Cash Inflows A = 60,000 + 70,000 + 80,000 + 90,000 = 3,00,000
•   Total Net Cash Inflows B = 80,000 + 90,000 + 1,00,000 + 1,20,000 = 3,90,000
• Depreciation A = 2,50,000 / 4 = 62,500 per year
• Depreciation B = 3,00,000 / 4 = 75,000 per year
Calculation of annual profit for both projects:
• Annual Profit A = (3,00,000 / 4) - 62,500 = 75,000 - 62,500 = 12,500
• Annual Profit B = (3,90,000 / 4) - 75,000 = 97,500 - 75,000 = 22,500
Calculation of Accounting Rate of Return (ARR)
ARR = (Average Annual Profit / Initial Investment) × 100
• ARR A = (12,500 / 2,50,000) × 100 = 5%
• ARR B = (22,500 / 3,00,000) × 100 = 7.5%
Payback Period is the time required to recover the initial investment from cash inflows.
Project A:
• Year 1 cumulative inflow = 60,000
• Year 2 cumulative inflow = 60,000 + 70,000 = 1,30,000
• Year 3 cumulative inflow = 1,30,000 + 80,000 = 2,10,000
• Year 4 cumulative inflow = 2,10,000 + 90,000 = 3,00,000
Initial investment: 2,50,000
Payback Period = 3 years + (40,000 / 90,000) = 3.44 years
Project B:
• Year 1 cumulative inflow = 80,000
• Year 2 cumulative inflow = 80,000 + 90,000 = 1,70,000
• Year 3 cumulative inflow = 1,70,000 + 1,00,000 = 2,70,000
• Year 4 cumulative inflow = 2,70,000 + 1,20,000 = 3,90,000
Initial investment: 3,00,000
Payback Period = 3 years + (30,000 / 1,20,000) = 3.25 years
Interpretation and Recommendation:
********** 7 **********
--- page 8 ---
•   Project B has a higher Accounting Rate of Return (7.5% vs. 5%) and a shorter payback period
(3.25 years vs. 3.44 years).
• This indicates Project B is financially more attractive with quicker recovery and greater average
profitability.
•   Therefore, Project B should be preferred over Project A.

6 b. A business requires funds for various activities such as starting operations, expanding, managing
working capital, or investing in fixed assets. The sources of funds are broadly classified as:
1. Internal Sources:
• Retained Earnings: Profits reinvested in the business rather than distributed as dividends.
•   Sale of Assets: Selling unused or obsolete assets to raise cash.
• Owners’ Capital: Money invested by business owners or shareholders.
2. External Sources:
• Equity Capital: Raising funds through issuing shares to investors, giving them ownership.
• Debt Financing: Borrowings from banks or financial institutions, such as loans,
debentures, and bonds.
• Trade Credit: Credit extended by suppliers, allowing delayed payment.
•   Factoring: Selling trade receivables to third parties for immediate cash.
• Grants and Subsidies: Funds from government or agencies, usually non-repayable.
• Crowdfunding: Raising small amounts from a large number of people via online
platforms.
7.
1. Total Market Equity = Equity capital + Reserves = ₹1,100 lakhs + ₹3,000 lakhs = ₹4,100 lakhs
2. Calculate Cost of Equity (Ke) using CAPM
Ke=Rf+β(Rm−Rf)
• Rf= 6%
• β = 1.5
• Rm−Rf = 7%
Ke=6%+1.5×7%=6%+10.5%=16.5%
3. Cost of Redeemable Debentures (Kd)
Debentures redeemable at par after 7 years, current price ₹980.
Kd=I+(RV−NP)/N/(RV+NP)/2
Where:
•   I = Coupon = 9% of ₹1,000 = ₹90 per debenture
• RV = Redemption Value = ₹1,000
• NP = Net Proceeds = ₹980
• N = Years = 7
Kd pre−tax=290+(1,000−980)/7/(1,000+980)/2 = 9.385%.
After-tax Kd (Tax Rate = 30%):
Kdafter−tax=9.385%×(1−0.30)=9.385%×0.70=6.57%
4. Cost of Preference Share Capital (Kp)
Redeemable at 5% premium after 5 years, price ₹105.
Kp=D+(RV−NP)/N/(RV+NP)/2
Kp=28+(105−105)/5/ (105+105)/2=8/105=7.62%
5. Cost of Term Loan (Kw): 11% × (1 − tax rate) = 11% × 0.70 = 7.7%
********** 9 **********
--- page 10 ---
6.
Source   Market   Weight
Value
(₹ lakhs)

and Reserves
Preference   1,050   0.121
Shares
Debentures   1,960   0.227
Term Loans   1,000   0.116
Total   8,110   1
WACC=(E/V)×Ke+(P/V)×Kp+(D/V)×Kd(after−tax)+(TL/V)×Kw
WACC=0.475×16.5%+0.121×7.62%+0.227×6.57%+0.116×7.7%
=7.8375%+0.9220%+1.490%+0.893%=11.14%
````

````text
company assets

lower than equity
Repayment   Must be repaid at   Usually redeemable after a certain
maturity   period
Priority at   Paid before preference   Paid after debenture holders but
Liquidation   shareholders   before equity shareholders
````

````text
Security   Usually secured against   Generally unsecured
company assets
Risk Level   Lower risk   Higher risk than debentures but
lower than equity
Repayment   Must be repaid at   Usually redeemable after a certain
maturity   period
Priority at   Paid before preference   Paid after debenture holders but
Liquidation   shareholders   before equity shareholders
********** 8 **********
````

````text
Q.6 b.   Advantages:   [10]
CO - 1   1. Lower Cost of Capital: Bonds usually have lower interest rates than
BL   equity financing reducing overall capital costs.
Remember   2. No Ownership Dilution: Unlike issuing shares bonds do not dilute
ownership or control of existing shareholders.
3. Tax Deductibility: Interest payments on bonds are tax-deductible
lowering the company’s taxable income.
4. Long-Term Stability: Bonds provide a stable and predictable source of
financing with fixed repayment schedules.
5. Flexibility in Structure: Companies can issue different types of bonds
(convertible callable secured unsecured) to match their financial strategy.
Disadvantages:
1. Fixed Interest Obligation: Interest payments must be made regardless of
profitability increasing financial burden.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
2. Debt Repayment Pressure: Principal repayment at maturity can create
liquidity stress for the company.
3. Increased Financial Risk: High reliance on bonds increases leverage and
raises default risk during economic downturns.
4. Restrictive Covenants: Bond agreements often include financial and
operational restrictions limiting company flexibility.
5. Credit Rating Sensitivity: A company’s ability to issue bonds at
favorable rates depends on its credit rating which can deteriorate due to
poor financial performance.
Q.7 a   Financial management plays a crucial role in impacting shareholder value and [10]
CO -1   wealth maximization by focusing on maximizing the value of the company.
BL   Impact on Shareholder Value and Wealth Maximization: (2 Marks)
Remember   Effective financial management ensures that the company generates sustainable
profits, which increase the value of the business over time. Growth in earnings leads
to higher dividends and an increase in the company’s stock price, benefiting
shareholders.
Financial managers focus on minimizing unnecessary costs and improving
operational efficiency. This, in turn, leads to better profit margins and more
available capital for reinvestment, driving future growth and increasing shareholder
wealth.
Key Decision Areas a Finance Manager Would Evaluate to Achieve Wealth
Maximization:
Investment Decisions: (2 Marks)
Capital Budgeting involves evaluating potential investment projects to ensure they
will generate returns that exceed their costs, thereby increasing the value of the
company. Finance managers assess projects using methods like Net Present Value
(NPV), Internal Rate of Return (IRR), and Payback Period.
The goal is to select investments that will generate positive returns and contribute
to long-term value creation.
Financing Decisions: (2 Marks)
Capital Structure decisions involve determining the optimal mix of debt and equity
financing. Finance managers analyze how much debt the company should take on,
balancing the cost of debt with the risk of financial distress.
A well-structured capital mix helps minimize the cost of capital, maximize return
on equity, and support sustainable growth, directly contributing to shareholder
wealth.
Dividend Policy Decisions: (2 Marks)
Dividend Policy entails deciding how much profit should be distributed to
shareholders as dividends and how much should be retained within the business to
fund future growth.
Finance managers need to balance the expectation of shareholders for immediate
returns and the need to reinvest in profitable growth opportunities. An appropriate
dividend policy enhances shareholder satisfaction and contributes to increasing
shareholder value.
Working Capital Management: (2 Marks)
Working Capital Management involves managing short-term assets and liabilities
efficiently, ensuring that the company has enough liquidity to meet its operational
needs while also using excess funds effectively for profitable investments.
Optimizing working capital ensures the company can continue its operations
smoothly, avoid disruptions, and invest in growth opportunities, which in turn
maximizes shareholder wealth.
Financial management’s primary objective of maximizing shareholder value and
wealth can be achieved by making informed and strategic decisions across various
areas like investment, financing, dividend policy, working capital management, and
risk management. These decisions ensure that the company remains profitable,
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
financially stable, and capable of sustainable growth, ultimately increasing the value
of the company and benefiting shareholders.
Q.7 b   a) Ans: Transaction Motive – To meet day-to-day operational expenses like   [10]
CO -1   salaries, rent, and raw material purchases.
BL   b) Precautionary Motive – To maintain cash reserves for unexpected
Remember   emergencies or financial uncertainties.
c) Speculative Motive – To take advantage of unexpected profitable
opportunities, such as discounted raw materials or favorable investment
conditions.
d) Compensating Balance Motive – To maintain the required minimum cash
balance as per bank agreements to avail banking services or loans.
********** 6 **********
````

````text
Q.6 b.   Advantages:   [10]
CO - 1   1. Lower Cost of Capital: Bonds usually have lower interest rates than
BL   equity financing reducing overall capital costs.
Remember   2. No Ownership Dilution: Unlike issuing shares bonds do not dilute
ownership or control of existing shareholders.
3. Tax Deductibility: Interest payments on bonds are tax-deductible
lowering the company’s taxable income.
4. Long-Term Stability: Bonds provide a stable and predictable source of
financing with fixed repayment schedules.
5. Flexibility in Structure: Companies can issue different types of bonds
(convertible callable secured unsecured) to match their financial strategy.
Disadvantages:
1. Fixed Interest Obligation: Interest payments must be made regardless of
profitability increasing financial burden.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
2. Debt Repayment Pressure: Principal repayment at maturity can create
liquidity stress for the company.
3. Increased Financial Risk: High reliance on bonds increases leverage and
raises default risk during economic downturns.
4. Restrictive Covenants: Bond agreements often include financial and
operational restrictions limiting company flexibility.
5. Credit Rating Sensitivity: A company’s ability to issue bonds at
favorable rates depends on its credit rating which can deteriorate due to
poor financial performance.
Q.7 a   Financial management plays a crucial role in impacting shareholder value and [10]
CO -1   wealth maximization by focusing on maximizing the value of the company.
BL   Impact on Shareholder Value and Wealth Maximization: (2 Marks)
Remember   Effective financial management ensures that the company generates sustainable
profits, which increase the value of the business over time. Growth in earnings leads
to higher dividends and an increase in the company’s stock price, benefiting
shareholders.
Financial managers focus on minimizing unnecessary costs and improving
operational efficiency. This, in turn, leads to better profit margins and more
available capital for reinvestment, driving future growth and increasing shareholder
wealth.
Key Decision Areas a Finance Manager Would Evaluate to Achieve Wealth
Maximization:
Investment Decisions: (2 Marks)
Capital Budgeting involves evaluating potential investment projects to ensure they
will generate returns that exceed their costs, thereby increasing the value of the
company. Finance managers assess projects using methods like Net Present Value
(NPV), Internal Rate of Return (IRR), and Payback Period.
The goal is to select investments that will generate positive returns and contribute
to long-term value creation.
Financing Decisions: (2 Marks)
Capital Structure decisions involve determining the optimal mix of debt and equity
financing. Finance managers analyze how much debt the company should take on,
balancing the cost of debt with the risk of financial distress.
A well-structured capital mix helps minimize the cost of capital, maximize return
on equity, and support sustainable growth, directly contributing to shareholder
wealth.
Dividend Policy Decisions: (2 Marks)
Dividend Policy entails deciding how much profit should be distributed to
shareholders as dividends and how much should be retained within the business to
fund future growth.
Finance managers need to balance the expectation of shareholders for immediate
returns and the need to reinvest in profitable growth opportunities. An appropriate
dividend policy enhances shareholder satisfaction and contributes to increasing
shareholder value.
Working Capital Management: (2 Marks)
Working Capital Management involves managing short-term assets and liabilities
efficiently, ensuring that the company has enough liquidity to meet its operational
needs while also using excess funds effectively for profitable investments.
Optimizing working capital ensures the company can continue its operations
smoothly, avoid disruptions, and invest in growth opportunities, which in turn
maximizes shareholder wealth.
Financial management’s primary objective of maximizing shareholder value and
wealth can be achieved by making informed and strategic decisions across various
areas like investment, financing, dividend policy, working capital management, and
risk management. These decisions ensure that the company remains profitable,
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
financially stable, and capable of sustainable growth, ultimately increasing the value
of the company and benefiting shareholders.
Q.7 b   a) Ans: Transaction Motive – To meet day-to-day operational expenses like   [10]
CO -1   salaries, rent, and raw material purchases.
BL   b) Precautionary Motive – To maintain cash reserves for unexpected
Remember   emergencies or financial uncertainties.
c) Speculative Motive – To take advantage of unexpected profitable
opportunities, such as discounted raw materials or favorable investment
conditions.
d) Compensating Balance Motive – To maintain the required minimum cash
balance as per bank agreements to avail banking services or loans.
********** 6 **********
````

````text
Q.6 b.   Advantages:   [10]
CO - 1   1. Lower Cost of Capital: Bonds usually have lower interest rates than
BL   equity financing reducing overall capital costs.
Remember   2. No Ownership Dilution: Unlike issuing shares bonds do not dilute
ownership or control of existing shareholders.
3. Tax Deductibility: Interest payments on bonds are tax-deductible
lowering the company’s taxable income.
4. Long-Term Stability: Bonds provide a stable and predictable source of
financing with fixed repayment schedules.
5. Flexibility in Structure: Companies can issue different types of bonds
(convertible callable secured unsecured) to match their financial strategy.
Disadvantages:
1. Fixed Interest Obligation: Interest payments must be made regardless of
profitability increasing financial burden.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
2. Debt Repayment Pressure: Principal repayment at maturity can create
liquidity stress for the company.
3. Increased Financial Risk: High reliance on bonds increases leverage and
raises default risk during economic downturns.
4. Restrictive Covenants: Bond agreements often include financial and
operational restrictions limiting company flexibility.
5. Credit Rating Sensitivity: A company’s ability to issue bonds at
favorable rates depends on its credit rating which can deteriorate due to
poor financial performance.
Q.7 a   Financial management plays a crucial role in impacting shareholder value and [10]
CO -1   wealth maximization by focusing on maximizing the value of the company.
BL   Impact on Shareholder Value and Wealth Maximization: (2 Marks)
Remember   Effective financial management ensures that the company generates sustainable
profits, which increase the value of the business over time. Growth in earnings leads
to higher dividends and an increase in the company’s stock price, benefiting
shareholders.
Financial managers focus on minimizing unnecessary costs and improving
operational efficiency. This, in turn, leads to better profit margins and more
available capital for reinvestment, driving future growth and increasing shareholder
wealth.
Key Decision Areas a Finance Manager Would Evaluate to Achieve Wealth
Maximization:
Investment Decisions: (2 Marks)
Capital Budgeting involves evaluating potential investment projects to ensure they
will generate returns that exceed their costs, thereby increasing the value of the
company. Finance managers assess projects using methods like Net Present Value
(NPV), Internal Rate of Return (IRR), and Payback Period.
The goal is to select investments that will generate positive returns and contribute
to long-term value creation.
Financing Decisions: (2 Marks)
Capital Structure decisions involve determining the optimal mix of debt and equity
financing. Finance managers analyze how much debt the company should take on,
balancing the cost of debt with the risk of financial distress.
A well-structured capital mix helps minimize the cost of capital, maximize return
on equity, and support sustainable growth, directly contributing to shareholder
wealth.
Dividend Policy Decisions: (2 Marks)
Dividend Policy entails deciding how much profit should be distributed to
shareholders as dividends and how much should be retained within the business to
fund future growth.
Finance managers need to balance the expectation of shareholders for immediate
returns and the need to reinvest in profitable growth opportunities. An appropriate
dividend policy enhances shareholder satisfaction and contributes to increasing
shareholder value.
Working Capital Management: (2 Marks)
Working Capital Management involves managing short-term assets and liabilities
efficiently, ensuring that the company has enough liquidity to meet its operational
needs while also using excess funds effectively for profitable investments.
Optimizing working capital ensures the company can continue its operations
smoothly, avoid disruptions, and invest in growth opportunities, which in turn
maximizes shareholder wealth.
Financial management’s primary objective of maximizing shareholder value and
wealth can be achieved by making informed and strategic decisions across various
areas like investment, financing, dividend policy, working capital management, and
risk management. These decisions ensure that the company remains profitable,
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
financially stable, and capable of sustainable growth, ultimately increasing the value
of the company and benefiting shareholders.
Q.7 b   a) Ans: Transaction Motive – To meet day-to-day operational expenses like   [10]
CO -1   salaries, rent, and raw material purchases.
BL   b) Precautionary Motive – To maintain cash reserves for unexpected
Remember   emergencies or financial uncertainties.
c) Speculative Motive – To take advantage of unexpected profitable
opportunities, such as discounted raw materials or favorable investment
conditions.
d) Compensating Balance Motive – To maintain the required minimum cash
balance as per bank agreements to avail banking services or loans.
********** 6 **********
````

````text
Q.6 b.   Advantages:   [10]
CO - 1   1. Lower Cost of Capital: Bonds usually have lower interest rates than
BL   equity financing reducing overall capital costs.
Remember   2. No Ownership Dilution: Unlike issuing shares bonds do not dilute
ownership or control of existing shareholders.
3. Tax Deductibility: Interest payments on bonds are tax-deductible
lowering the company’s taxable income.
4. Long-Term Stability: Bonds provide a stable and predictable source of
financing with fixed repayment schedules.
5. Flexibility in Structure: Companies can issue different types of bonds
(convertible callable secured unsecured) to match their financial strategy.
Disadvantages:
1. Fixed Interest Obligation: Interest payments must be made regardless of
profitability increasing financial burden.
********** 4 **********
--- page 5 ---
2. Debt Repayment Pressure: Principal repayment at maturity can create
liquidity stress for the company.
3. Increased Financial Risk: High reliance on bonds increases leverage and
raises default risk during economic downturns.
4. Restrictive Covenants: Bond agreements often include financial and
operational restrictions limiting company flexibility.
5. Credit Rating Sensitivity: A company’s ability to issue bonds at
favorable rates depends on its credit rating which can deteriorate due to
poor financial performance.
Q.7 a   Financial management plays a crucial role in impacting shareholder value and [10]
CO -1   wealth maximization by focusing on maximizing the value of the company.
BL   Impact on Shareholder Value and Wealth Maximization: (2 Marks)
Remember   Effective financial management ensures that the company generates sustainable
profits, which increase the value of the business over time. Growth in earnings leads
to higher dividends and an increase in the company’s stock price, benefiting
shareholders.
Financial managers focus on minimizing unnecessary costs and improving
operational efficiency. This, in turn, leads to better profit margins and more
available capital for reinvestment, driving future growth and increasing shareholder
wealth.
Key Decision Areas a Finance Manager Would Evaluate to Achieve Wealth
Maximization:
Investment Decisions: (2 Marks)
Capital Budgeting involves evaluating potential investment projects to ensure they
will generate returns that exceed their costs, thereby increasing the value of the
company. Finance managers assess projects using methods like Net Present Value
(NPV), Internal Rate of Return (IRR), and Payback Period.
The goal is to select investments that will generate positive returns and contribute
to long-term value creation.
Financing Decisions: (2 Marks)
Capital Structure decisions involve determining the optimal mix of debt and equity
financing. Finance managers analyze how much debt the company should take on,
balancing the cost of debt with the risk of financial distress.
A well-structured capital mix helps minimize the cost of capital, maximize return
on equity, and support sustainable growth, directly contributing to shareholder
wealth.
Dividend Policy Decisions: (2 Marks)
Dividend Policy entails deciding how much profit should be distributed to
shareholders as dividends and how much should be retained within the business to
fund future growth.
Finance managers need to balance the expectation of shareholders for immediate
returns and the need to reinvest in profitable growth opportunities. An appropriate
dividend policy enhances shareholder satisfaction and contributes to increasing
shareholder value.
Working Capital Management: (2 Marks)
Working Capital Management involves managing short-term assets and liabilities
efficiently, ensuring that the company has enough liquidity to meet its operational
needs while also using excess funds effectively for profitable investments.
Optimizing working capital ensures the company can continue its operations
smoothly, avoid disruptions, and invest in growth opportunities, which in turn
maximizes shareholder wealth.
Financial management’s primary objective of maximizing shareholder value and
wealth can be achieved by making informed and strategic decisions across various
areas like investment, financing, dividend policy, working capital management, and
risk management. These decisions ensure that the company remains profitable,
********** 5 **********
--- page 6 ---
financially stable, and capable of sustainable growth, ultimately increasing the value
of the company and benefiting shareholders.
Q.7 b   a) Ans: Transaction Motive – To meet day-to-day operational expenses like   [10]
CO -1   salaries, rent, and raw material purchases.
BL   b) Precautionary Motive – To maintain cash reserves for unexpected
Remember   emergencies or financial uncertainties.
c) Speculative Motive – To take advantage of unexpected profitable
opportunities, such as discounted raw materials or favorable investment
conditions.
d) Compensating Balance Motive – To maintain the required minimum cash
balance as per bank agreements to avail banking services or loans.
********** 6 **********
````

````text
Q.1   a Primary Market:   [05]
CO -1   . The primary market is the financial market where new securities, such as shares and
BL -   bonds, are issued for the first time by companies, governments, or institutions to raise
Remem   capital. Investors purchase these securities directly from the issuer through mechanisms
ber   like Initial Public Offerings (IPOs), private placements, and rights issues. The primary
market plays a crucial role in capital formation, enabling businesses to finance
expansion, research, and development. Since securities are sold for the first time, there is
no prior trading history, and funds raised go directly to the issuing entity.
Secondary Market:
The secondary market is where previously issued securities are bought and sold among
investors. Unlike the primary market, transactions in the secondary market do not
involve the issuing company; instead, securities trade on stock exchanges (e.g., NSE,
BSE) or over the counter (OTC) markets. This market enhances liquidity, allowing
investors to sell their holdings easily, and facilitates price discovery based on demand
and supply. The secondary market also promotes investor confidence, as it provides an
opportunity to trade securities after their initial issuance.

Q.7 a   Agency Conflict refers to the conflict of interest that arises when there is a separation   [10]
CO -1   between the ownership and control of a business. It typically occurs between principals
BL   (shareholders or owners) and agents (managers or executives) who are hired to manage
Remem   the organization. The principals expect the agents to act in their best interests, but
ber   because agents may have their own interests or personal goals, their actions may not
always align with the principals' objectives. (2 Marks)
Reasons Behind Agency Conflict: (4 Marks)
Different Goals and Interests:
Shareholders (principals) generally aim to maximize the value of their investment and
receive high returns. Managers (agents), on the other hand, may focus on personal goals
such as job security, higher salaries, or perks, which may not always align with the goal
of maximizing shareholder wealth.
Information Asymmetry:
Managers often have more information about the company's operations than
shareholders. This information imbalance can allow managers to make decisions that
benefit themselves rather than the owners of the company.
Risk Preferences:
Shareholders may be willing to take on more risk for higher returns, while managers,
with less at stake in terms of personal wealth, may be more risk-averse and prefer safer,
less profitable investments that protect their position.
Short-Term vs. Long-Term Goals:
Shareholders might prioritize long-term growth, while managers could focus on short-
term results to enhance their compensation (e.g., through annual bonuses or stock
options), which can lead to decisions that do not benefit the company in the long run.
Ways to Overcome Agency Conflict: (4 Marks)
Incentive Alignment:
Aligning the interests of managers with those of shareholders can help reduce agency
conflicts. This can be done by offering performance-based compensation, such as stock
options, bonuses, or profit-sharing plans, which motivate managers to act in the best
interests of the shareholders.
Effective Monitoring:
Regular monitoring of managerial actions through mechanisms such as audits, board of
directors' oversight, and performance evaluations can help ensure that managers are
acting in the best interest of the shareholders.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Transparent Reporting and Information Sharing:
Encouraging transparent communication and regular reporting of financial performance
helps reduce information asymmetry between managers and shareholders, making it
easier to track whether managers are acting in the company’s best interest.
Corporate Governance:
Strong corporate governance practices, such as having an independent board of directors,
can help mitigate agency problems. The board should oversee management decisions to
ensure they align with the interests of shareholders.
By addressing the root causes of agency conflict and implementing these strategies,
organizations can reduce conflicts and improve decision-making to maximize
shareholder value.
Q.7 b   a) Collection Cost – Expenses incurred in managing and collecting receivables,   [10]
CO -1   such as billing, reminders, follow-ups, and collection agency fees.
BL   b) Capital Cost – The opportunity cost of funds tied up in receivables, which could
Remem   have been invested elsewhere for returns.
ber   c) Delinquency Cost – The cost arising due to delayed payments, including loss of
interest income and administrative expenses for tracking overdue accounts.
d) Default Cost – Losses incurred when customers fail to pay their dues, leading to
bad debts and provisions for doubtful accounts.
********** 7 **********
````

````text
Q.7 a   Agency Conflict refers to the conflict of interest that arises when there is a separation   [10]
CO -1   between the ownership and control of a business. It typically occurs between principals
BL   (shareholders or owners) and agents (managers or executives) who are hired to manage
Remem   the organization. The principals expect the agents to act in their best interests, but
ber   because agents may have their own interests or personal goals, their actions may not
always align with the principals' objectives. (2 Marks)
Reasons Behind Agency Conflict: (4 Marks)
Different Goals and Interests:
Shareholders (principals) generally aim to maximize the value of their investment and
receive high returns. Managers (agents), on the other hand, may focus on personal goals
such as job security, higher salaries, or perks, which may not always align with the goal
of maximizing shareholder wealth.
Information Asymmetry:
Managers often have more information about the company's operations than
shareholders. This information imbalance can allow managers to make decisions that
benefit themselves rather than the owners of the company.
Risk Preferences:
Shareholders may be willing to take on more risk for higher returns, while managers,
with less at stake in terms of personal wealth, may be more risk-averse and prefer safer,
less profitable investments that protect their position.
Short-Term vs. Long-Term Goals:
Shareholders might prioritize long-term growth, while managers could focus on short-
term results to enhance their compensation (e.g., through annual bonuses or stock
options), which can lead to decisions that do not benefit the company in the long run.
Ways to Overcome Agency Conflict: (4 Marks)
Incentive Alignment:
Aligning the interests of managers with those of shareholders can help reduce agency
conflicts. This can be done by offering performance-based compensation, such as stock
options, bonuses, or profit-sharing plans, which motivate managers to act in the best
interests of the shareholders.
Effective Monitoring:
Regular monitoring of managerial actions through mechanisms such as audits, board of
directors' oversight, and performance evaluations can help ensure that managers are
acting in the best interest of the shareholders.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Transparent Reporting and Information Sharing:
Encouraging transparent communication and regular reporting of financial performance
helps reduce information asymmetry between managers and shareholders, making it
easier to track whether managers are acting in the company’s best interest.
Corporate Governance:
Strong corporate governance practices, such as having an independent board of directors,
can help mitigate agency problems. The board should oversee management decisions to
ensure they align with the interests of shareholders.
By addressing the root causes of agency conflict and implementing these strategies,
organizations can reduce conflicts and improve decision-making to maximize
shareholder value.
Q.7 b   a) Collection Cost – Expenses incurred in managing and collecting receivables,   [10]
CO -1   such as billing, reminders, follow-ups, and collection agency fees.
BL   b) Capital Cost – The opportunity cost of funds tied up in receivables, which could
Remem   have been invested elsewhere for returns.
ber   c) Delinquency Cost – The cost arising due to delayed payments, including loss of
interest income and administrative expenses for tracking overdue accounts.
d) Default Cost – Losses incurred when customers fail to pay their dues, leading to
bad debts and provisions for doubtful accounts.
********** 7 **********
````

````text
Q.1   a Primary Market:   [05]
CO -1   . The primary market is the financial market where new securities, such as shares and
BL -   bonds, are issued for the first time by companies, governments, or institutions to raise
Remem   capital. Investors purchase these securities directly from the issuer through mechanisms
ber   like Initial Public Offerings (IPOs), private placements, and rights issues. The primary
market plays a crucial role in capital formation, enabling businesses to finance
expansion, research, and development. Since securities are sold for the first time, there is
no prior trading history, and funds raised go directly to the issuing entity.
Secondary Market:
The secondary market is where previously issued securities are bought and sold among
investors. Unlike the primary market, transactions in the secondary market do not
involve the issuing company; instead, securities trade on stock exchanges (e.g., NSE,
BSE) or over the counter (OTC) markets. This market enhances liquidity, allowing
investors to sell their holdings easily, and facilitates price discovery based on demand
and supply. The secondary market also promotes investor confidence, as it provides an
opportunity to trade securities after their initial issuance.

Q.7 a   Agency Conflict refers to the conflict of interest that arises when there is a separation   [10]
CO -1   between the ownership and control of a business. It typically occurs between principals
BL   (shareholders or owners) and agents (managers or executives) who are hired to manage
Remem   the organization. The principals expect the agents to act in their best interests, but
ber   because agents may have their own interests or personal goals, their actions may not
always align with the principals' objectives. (2 Marks)
Reasons Behind Agency Conflict: (4 Marks)
Different Goals and Interests:
Shareholders (principals) generally aim to maximize the value of their investment and
receive high returns. Managers (agents), on the other hand, may focus on personal goals
such as job security, higher salaries, or perks, which may not always align with the goal
of maximizing shareholder wealth.
Information Asymmetry:
Managers often have more information about the company's operations than
shareholders. This information imbalance can allow managers to make decisions that
benefit themselves rather than the owners of the company.
Risk Preferences:
Shareholders may be willing to take on more risk for higher returns, while managers,
with less at stake in terms of personal wealth, may be more risk-averse and prefer safer,
less profitable investments that protect their position.
Short-Term vs. Long-Term Goals:
Shareholders might prioritize long-term growth, while managers could focus on short-
term results to enhance their compensation (e.g., through annual bonuses or stock
options), which can lead to decisions that do not benefit the company in the long run.
Ways to Overcome Agency Conflict: (4 Marks)
Incentive Alignment:
Aligning the interests of managers with those of shareholders can help reduce agency
conflicts. This can be done by offering performance-based compensation, such as stock
options, bonuses, or profit-sharing plans, which motivate managers to act in the best
interests of the shareholders.
Effective Monitoring:
Regular monitoring of managerial actions through mechanisms such as audits, board of
directors' oversight, and performance evaluations can help ensure that managers are
acting in the best interest of the shareholders.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Transparent Reporting and Information Sharing:
Encouraging transparent communication and regular reporting of financial performance
helps reduce information asymmetry between managers and shareholders, making it
easier to track whether managers are acting in the company’s best interest.
Corporate Governance:
Strong corporate governance practices, such as having an independent board of directors,
can help mitigate agency problems. The board should oversee management decisions to
ensure they align with the interests of shareholders.
By addressing the root causes of agency conflict and implementing these strategies,
organizations can reduce conflicts and improve decision-making to maximize
shareholder value.
Q.7 b   a) Collection Cost – Expenses incurred in managing and collecting receivables,   [10]
CO -1   such as billing, reminders, follow-ups, and collection agency fees.
BL   b) Capital Cost – The opportunity cost of funds tied up in receivables, which could
Remem   have been invested elsewhere for returns.
ber   c) Delinquency Cost – The cost arising due to delayed payments, including loss of
interest income and administrative expenses for tracking overdue accounts.
d) Default Cost – Losses incurred when customers fail to pay their dues, leading to
bad debts and provisions for doubtful accounts.
********** 7 **********
````

````text
CO -1   . The primary market is the financial market where new securities, such as shares and
BL -   bonds, are issued for the first time by companies, governments, or institutions to raise
Remem   capital. Investors purchase these securities directly from the issuer through mechanisms
ber   like Initial Public Offerings (IPOs), private placements, and rights issues. The primary
market plays a crucial role in capital formation, enabling businesses to finance
expansion, research, and development. Since securities are sold for the first time, there is
no prior trading history, and funds raised go directly to the issuing entity.
Secondary Market:
The secondary market is where previously issued securities are bought and sold among
investors. Unlike the primary market, transactions in the secondary market do not
involve the issuing company; instead, securities trade on stock exchanges (e.g., NSE,
BSE) or over the counter (OTC) markets. This market enhances liquidity, allowing
investors to sell their holdings easily, and facilitates price discovery based on demand
and supply. The secondary market also promotes investor confidence, as it provides an
opportunity to trade securities after their initial issuance.

Q.7 a   Agency Conflict refers to the conflict of interest that arises when there is a separation   [10]
CO -1   between the ownership and control of a business. It typically occurs between principals
BL   (shareholders or owners) and agents (managers or executives) who are hired to manage
Remem   the organization. The principals expect the agents to act in their best interests, but
ber   because agents may have their own interests or personal goals, their actions may not
always align with the principals' objectives. (2 Marks)
Reasons Behind Agency Conflict: (4 Marks)
Different Goals and Interests:
Shareholders (principals) generally aim to maximize the value of their investment and
receive high returns. Managers (agents), on the other hand, may focus on personal goals
such as job security, higher salaries, or perks, which may not always align with the goal
of maximizing shareholder wealth.
Information Asymmetry:
Managers often have more information about the company's operations than
shareholders. This information imbalance can allow managers to make decisions that
benefit themselves rather than the owners of the company.
Risk Preferences:
Shareholders may be willing to take on more risk for higher returns, while managers,
with less at stake in terms of personal wealth, may be more risk-averse and prefer safer,
less profitable investments that protect their position.
Short-Term vs. Long-Term Goals:
Shareholders might prioritize long-term growth, while managers could focus on short-
term results to enhance their compensation (e.g., through annual bonuses or stock
options), which can lead to decisions that do not benefit the company in the long run.
Ways to Overcome Agency Conflict: (4 Marks)
Incentive Alignment:
Aligning the interests of managers with those of shareholders can help reduce agency
conflicts. This can be done by offering performance-based compensation, such as stock
options, bonuses, or profit-sharing plans, which motivate managers to act in the best
interests of the shareholders.
Effective Monitoring:
Regular monitoring of managerial actions through mechanisms such as audits, board of
directors' oversight, and performance evaluations can help ensure that managers are
acting in the best interest of the shareholders.
********** 6 **********
--- page 7 ---
Transparent Reporting and Information Sharing:
Encouraging transparent communication and regular reporting of financial performance
helps reduce information asymmetry between managers and shareholders, making it
easier to track whether managers are acting in the company’s best interest.
Corporate Governance:
Strong corporate governance practices, such as having an independent board of directors,
can help mitigate agency problems. The board should oversee management decisions to
ensure they align with the interests of shareholders.
By addressing the root causes of agency conflict and implementing these strategies,
organizations can reduce conflicts and improve decision-making to maximize
shareholder value.
Q.7 b   a) Collection Cost – Expenses incurred in managing and collecting receivables,   [10]
CO -1   such as billing, reminders, follow-ups, and collection agency fees.
BL   b) Capital Cost – The opportunity cost of funds tied up in receivables, which could
Remem   have been invested elsewhere for returns.
ber   c) Delinquency Cost – The cost arising due to delayed payments, including loss of
interest income and administrative expenses for tracking overdue accounts.
d) Default Cost – Losses incurred when customers fail to pay their dues, leading to
bad debts and provisions for doubtful accounts.
********** 7 **********
````

````text
Q.1   a Primary Market:   [05]
CO -1   . The primary market is the financial market where new securities, such as shares and
BL -   bonds, are issued for the first time by companies, governments, or institutions to raise
Remem   capital. Investors purchase these securities directly from the issuer through mechanisms
ber   like Initial Public Offerings (IPOs), private placements, and rights issues. The primary
market plays a crucial role in capital formation, enabling businesses to finance
expansion, research, and development. Since securities are sold for the first time, there is
no prior trading history, and funds raised go directly to the issuing entity.
Secondary Market:
The secondary market is where previously issued securities are bought and sold among
investors. Unlike the primary market, transactions in the secondary market do not
involve the issuing company; instead, securities trade on stock exchanges (e.g., NSE,
BSE) or over the counter (OTC) markets. This market enhances liquidity, allowing
investors to sell their holdings easily, and facilitates price discovery based on demand
and supply. The secondary market also promotes investor confidence, as it provides an
opportunity to trade securities after their initial issuance.

Different Goals and Interests:
Shareholders (principals) generally aim to maximize the value of their investment and
````
